--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission requires robots to **stop before the switch time** and **move after the switch time**. The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up. Before the switch, stopping is rewarded (score penalizes moving robots). After the switch, moving is rewarded (score penalizes stationary robots).

**Key insight from results:**
- Best score (10095.61): Simply `Stop` forever (1 state, B=1). This is surprisingly good because it handles the pre-switch phase perfectly, and the post-switch penalty is bounded.
- The score of ~10095 suggests: ~500 steps × 20 robots stationary before switch = 10000, plus some post-switch penalty for not moving. The post-switch phase (600-800 steps remaining) costs ~12000-16000 if all robots stay still, but the champion gets ~10095, meaning some robots do move slightly or the switch happens late.

Wait - let me reconsider. If all robots stop: before switch (~500 steps avg) × 20 = 10000 penalty. After switch (~700 steps avg) × 20 = 14000 penalty. Total ≈ 24000. But champion gets 10095... 

Actually: pre-switch penalty = steps_before × moving_robots, post-switch penalty = steps_after × stationary_robots. Stop-always: pre-switch = 0 (stationary), post-switch = steps_after × 20. With avg switch at 500, post-switch ≈ 700 steps × 20 = 14000. But score is 10095... Maybe the switch time averaging gives lower values, or robots drift slightly.

Actually re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary" before switch. So Stop-all gives maximum pre-switch penalty! But it scored best... 

Oh wait - before switch: score += 1 per STATIONARY robot. After switch: score += 1 per MOVING robot. So ideally: move before switch, stop after switch. But the champion (all stop) scored 10095 while exploration scored 11621. So stopping is better than exploring? That means the post-switch movement penalty dominates.

Actually with all-stop: pre-switch ≈ 500×20=10000, post-switch ≈ 0 (all stopped). Total ≈ 10000. That matches! And with all-move: pre-switch ≈ 0, post-switch ≈ 700×20=14000. Total ≈ 14000. So **Stop** is near-optimal for the pre-switch phase, and after the switch we need to detect the green light and start moving.

**Optimal strategy**: Stop by default, then when GREEN wall is detected (C=7, color=2 for green), switch to moving (Exploration or GoToColor).

Let me design machines:
- State 0: Stop (B=1), transition on seeing green (C=7, l=2) → State 1
- State 1: Exploration (B=0), maybe transition back if green disappears

Green color in ARGoS is color index 2 (from the clr parameter descriptions: {1,2,3,4,5,6}).

**My machines:**

1. **Champion** (exact copy): 1-state Stop
2. **2-state Stop→Move on green**: Stop, transition to Exploration when green seen
3. **2-state Stop→Move on green**: Stop, transition to GoToColor(green) when green seen
4. Variations with different probabilities for the green detection
5. 3-state machines with more nuance

FSM candidates:
- Stop + detect green → explore
- Stop + detect green → GoToColor green (move toward it)
- Stop + detect green (high prob) → explore + return if no green
- 3-state: Stop → (on green) Move → (on green) GoToColor

Let me write 14 machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**

The champion is still the simple 1-state Stop (B=1) at 9697.62. This confirms: stopping always = ~9700 score (pre-switch penalty ~9700 over ~500 steps × ~20 robots? No... wait).

Let me recalculate: If all robots stop for 1200 steps, pre-switch penalty = switch_time × 20, post-switch penalty = 0. With switch uniform [400,600], avg = 500 steps × 20 robots = 10000 pre-switch penalty. Score ~9697 is consistent with this.

The 2-state machines (Stop → on green → Explore) scored 10503-11873, which is WORSE than pure stop. This means the green detection is causing robots to start moving, but it's not saving enough post-switch penalty to compensate for the pre-switch penalty they incur (if they falsely trigger before switch, or the transition fires too early).

Wait - actually the green light only appears AT the switch time. So if robots stop before switch (0 pre-switch penalty from stationary robots... wait no).

Re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**" before switch. So stationary = bad before switch! And moving = bad after switch.

So pure Stop gives: pre-switch_steps × 20 penalty + 0 post-switch penalty ≈ 500×20 = 10000. That matches ~9697.

Pure Move gives: 0 pre-switch + post_switch_steps × 20 ≈ 700×20 = 14000. That matches ~14000 scores.

**Ideal**: Move before switch, Stop after switch. Detect green light (appears at switch) to stop.

So the FSM should be:
- State 0: **Explore/Move** (reduces pre-switch penalty)  
- Transition: when green detected → State 1: **Stop** (reduces post-switch penalty)

But the 2-state machines tried Stop→Move on green, which is backwards! They start stopped (accumulating pre-switch penalty) then move after seeing green (accumulating post-switch penalty). That's actually the WRONG direction!

Wait, but those scored ~10503 which is better than pure exploration (~11621 from round 1). So maybe the green detection at prob=1.0 with low return rate works because they stop quickly after switch?

Actually: Stop→(see green)→Move: Before switch, all stopped = pre_switch×20 penalty. After switch, see green and start moving = low post_switch penalty. Score = pre_switch×20 + small_post = ~10000 + small. Champion gets 9697 because there's no post-switch movement penalty at all.

**The correct design**: Move→(see green)→Stop
- Before switch: robots move → 0 pre-switch penalty
- After switch: green appears, robots detect and stop → small post-switch penalty

This could score much better than ~9697 if robots can reliably detect the green and stop quickly!

Let me design these machines:

**Strategy A**: Explore → (see green, high prob) → Stop
**Strategy B**: Exploration → (see green) → Stop, with some return probability
**Strategy C**: Various movement behaviors → Stop on green

The key insight is: green color = 2 in the color codes. We want C=7 (perceive color), l=2 (green), high probability to transition.

For the move state, low rwm (less random turning) might be better to cover more ground and more likely to see the green wall.

Let me also consider: not all robots will see the green wall immediately. Robots far from it or facing away might not detect it. So we need a high enough detection probability and maybe a secondary trigger.

**Machines to try:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Explore → Stop on green (p=1.0)
3. Explore → Stop on green (p=0.9) + small random stop
4. Explore (low rwm) → Stop on green
5. Explore (high rwm) → Stop on green  
6. 3-state: Explore → (green) Stop → (no green, random) Explore
7. Attraction → Stop on green (clusters might see green better)
8. Repulsion → Stop on green (spread out to detect green faster)
9. Explore → Stop on green + neighbor condition backup
10. GoToColor(green) initially then stop? No - we want to move before switch

## Reasoning

**Key findings from rounds 1-3:**

The strategy of **Explore → (see green) → Stop** is confirmed as the right approach. The champion uses `rwm=10` (very low turning rate = mostly straight movement) which scored 9273. Lower rwm values seem better, suggesting robots that move more straightly can better cover the arena and detect the green wall.

The pattern is clear:
- State 0: Exploration (B=0) with low rwm → moves around, accumulating 0 pre-switch penalty
- Transition: C=7 (see green color=2) at p=1.0 → transition to Stop
- State 1: Stop (B=1) → accumulates 0 post-switch penalty

**Why rwm=10 wins over rwm=50,70,100**: Lower turning rate means robots move more in straight lines, covering more arena area, and more likely to face/approach the green wall. Actually, it might be that with rwm=10, robots move more predictably and detect the green wall faster.

**What to explore:**
1. Even lower rwm values (rwm=1, rwm=5)
2. The return transition probability matters: p=0.001 is near-zero (essentially never return). Maybe p=0 effectively... but minimum is 0.001 in practice.
3. The second-ranked machine has an extra transition in the Stop state: `c1x1 7 p1x1 0.02 l1x1 2` (if you see green again, go back to moving). This scored 9454, slightly worse than champion. This extra transition causes some robots to re-enter moving state post-switch.
4. Try different return probabilities from Stop state (smaller = better, but 0.001 might already be at floor)
5. Try adding a neighbor-based transition or random transition to help robots that miss the green signal

**Key parameters to tune:**
- rwm: Try 1, 3, 5, 7, 10 (champion), 15, 20
- Return prob from Stop: 0.0001? 0.001 (champion)
- Additional transitions: maybe a very small random probability to move (to escape stuck states), or neighbor count condition

**Machines for round 4:**

1. Champion (exact copy) - rwm=10
2. rwm=1 (even straighter)
3. rwm=3
4. rwm=5
5. rwm=7
6. rwm=15
7. rwm=20 (slightly more turning)
8. rwm=10 with extra random transition from Stop (very low p)
9. rwm=10 with return prob=0.0001 (even lower)
10. 3-state: Explore → Stop (on green) with a backup random-start exploration state
11. rwm=5 with two transitions from explore (green + very rare random stop)
12. Repulsion → Stop (spread out = better coverage = faster green detection)
13. rwm=10, stop state with neighbor-count trigger back to explore
14. GoToColor green initially... wait, that would stop moving. No. 

Actually for #14, let me try: Explore with very low rwm + the stop state having essentially zero return probability by making the condition nearly impossible to fire.

Let me also consider: the stop state having transition p=0.001 on C=5 (random) fires ~once per 1000 steps. Over 700 post-switch steps, P(fire) = 1-(1-0.001)^700 ≈ 50%. So half the runs will have robots returning to explore. Lower would be better. But 0.001 is probably already near minimum practical value.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from round 4:**

The champion is: `rwm=1, cle=4, 2 transitions from explore (green detection p=1.0 + random p=0.001)` → Stop. Score: 6467.85.

**Clear pattern emerging:**
- Lower rwm is better (1 > 3 > 5 > 7 > 10 > 15 > 20)
- Having 2 transitions from explore state (green + random p=0.001) is better than 1 transition (6467 vs 7472 for rwm=1)
- LED color 4 (yellow) on explore state performed slightly better than color 5 (magenta)
- The stop state with neighbor-count transition back (w=18) was catastrophic (rank 14)
- Repulsion as pre-switch behavior was bad

**Why rwm=1 works best**: rwm=1 means robots turn very rarely, essentially moving in nearly straight lines. This means they sweep across the arena efficiently and are more likely to pass near the green-lit wall and detect it.

**What to explore next:**
1. **rwm=1 with variations**: The champion has rwm=1, cle=4, 2 transitions. Try slight variations around this.
2. **Can rwm go lower?** rwm=1 is the minimum (integer in [1..100])
3. **The extra transition from explore**: p=0.001 on C=5 (random). What does this do? It causes robots to occasionally stop even before seeing green. This might help in some cases where robots are stuck in corners... but it seems beneficial.
4. **Stop state return transition**: p=0.001 means very rare return. Is this optimal? 
5. **3-state ideas**: explore(rwm=1) → stop → (something else?). The 3-state machine with rwm=3 and rwm=10 scored 8825, worse than champion.
6. **Try different LED colors** on explore state - might affect visibility?
7. **Try GoToColor(green)** as a secondary behavior after detecting green? No - we want to stop.
8. **Can we add a neighbor-based backup?** If many robots have already stopped (post-switch), detecting many stopped neighbors could trigger stopping too.

**The scoring math**: With rwm=1 (nearly straight), robots cross the arena quickly, hit walls, and face the green wall relatively quickly after switch. Score 6467 ≈ 500 steps × 20 robots × fraction_moving_before_switch_that_happens_to_stop_early... Actually: pre-switch cost = 0 (all moving), post-switch cost = detection_delay × 20. Score 6467 / 20 ≈ 323 steps average detection delay. That seems quite good for a 700-step post-switch window.

**Key explorations:**
1. Champion exact copy
2. rwm=1, cle=4, 2 transitions, but stop state also has a neighbor condition (many stopped neighbors → start moving? No... we want to stay stopped)
3. rwm=1, different return probabilities from stop (0.0001 - even lower)
4. rwm=1, cle=0 (off LED)
5. rwm=1, cle=6 
6. rwm=1, 3 transitions from explore (green + random + neighbor)
7. 3-state: rwm=1 → stop(on green) with a third state for robots that see green but are exploring
8. rwm=1 with stop state having no return transition at all? Can't - must have at least 1 transition. Use p=0.001 minimum.
9. Try adding GoToColor(green) as intermediate state before stopping
10. Try different explore behaviors before green is seen

The champion has `--n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.001` - this random transition from explore to stop fires at p=0.001 per step. Over 500 pre-switch steps, P(firing) = 1-(0.999)^500 ≈ 39%. This means ~8 robots stop randomly before switch, adding ~8×remaining_steps to score. Hmm, this might actually hurt. But it scored better than the version without it. Perhaps because robots that are stuck in corners can escape to stop state?

Actually wait - the random transition fires FROM explore TO stop. Before the switch, stopping is penalized. So this transition causes some robots to stop early (bad). But it still scored 6467 vs 7472 for the version without. Interesting. Maybe the stochastic stopping helps because once stopped, the robot doesn't move, so post-switch it's already stopped and doesn't incur post-switch penalty for that robot. It's a tradeoff.

Let me try p=0.0001 (even lower random stop) and see if it's better.

Also: the champion has `cle=4` (yellow LED) on explore state. Can robots see each other's LEDs and use that as a signal? The camera condition C=7 can detect LED colors. So if stopping robots emit green (cle=6), exploring robots could detect many green-LED robots (meaning switch happened) and stop too! This is a peer-signaling mechanism.

**Peer signaling idea**: 
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1), transition on: (a) see green wall (C=7, l=2) OR (b) see many green-LED robots (C=7, l=... wait, what's the LED color for green? cle=2 would be green LED)
- State 1: Stop with cle=2 (green LED)

If stopping robots emit green LED (cle=2), then other robots can detect the spread of the stop signal! This could significantly improve performance by propagating the stop behavior.

Let me try: explore → stop(cle=2) where stop robots emit green LED, and explore robots transition on seeing green (which could be wall OR other stopped robots).

But the green wall is also green... so C=7 l=2 could trigger on either wall green or robot green LED. This might cause premature stopping if some robots randomly emit green before the switch... but no robot starts in stop state, so initially no green LEDs. After switch, the wall lights up green AND gradually robots stop and emit green LEDs, reinforcing the signal.

This is a very promising idea!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from round 5:**

The champion is: `rwm=1, cle=4, 2 transitions from explore (green p=1.0 + random p=0.001)` → Stop with `2 transitions (random p=0.001 + neighbor-count C=3 p=5 w=15)`. Score: 6237.61.

**Critical insight**: The stop state having a neighbor-count condition (C=3, threshold=5, steepness=15) is the NEW key improvement! This means: if there are ≥5 neighbors moving around (which would happen post-switch if robots haven't stopped yet), the stopped robot... wait, C=3 fires when neighbors are ABOVE threshold. But stopped robots have 0 motion - why would neighbors being above 5 cause the stopped robot to do something?

Actually, re-reading: C=3 is "number of neighbours ABOVE threshold" - this transition goes FROM stop state BACK to explore. With p=5 (threshold=5 neighbors) and w=15 (steep sigmoid), this fires when ≥5 robots are nearby. But we don't want stopped robots to start moving again...

Wait, but it scored BETTER (6237 vs 6337). Maybe this condition rarely fires post-switch (few robots near each other when stopped), but helps pre-switch somehow? Or maybe the p=5 threshold means it almost never fires since robots are spread out (rwm=1 = straight lines = spread out), so it's essentially a no-op that doesn't hurt.

Actually, looking more carefully: ranks 2-7 all scored exactly 6337.85 - they're the same score despite different LED colors and one having rwm=2. This suggests there's a floor around 6337 for the basic 2-state design, and the champion's extra neighbor transition gives a small but real improvement.

**What does the neighbor-count-in-stop help?** If a robot is stopped post-switch, and many neighbors are also stopped (count BELOW threshold), it stays stopped. C=3 (above threshold) with p=5 neighbors: if many moving robots surround it, it transitions... but back to explore? That seems bad. Unless...

Actually wait: the transition FROM stop state goes to the ONLY other state (explore). So if ≥5 neighbors (moving, since robots either move or are stopped), it transitions to explore. This is counterproductive post-switch. Unless the real benefit is: if a robot stopped BEFORE the switch (via the p=0.001 random transition), and it sees many moving neighbors (everyone else still exploring), it goes back to exploring. This would reduce the pre-switch stop penalty!

**This makes sense!** The neighbor-count condition in stop state acts as a "mistake correction": robots that accidentally stopped pre-switch (via random p=0.001) get released back to exploring when they see many moving neighbors.

**Explorations:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Vary the neighbor threshold in stop state (3, 4, 5, 6, 7)
3. Vary the steepness w in stop state (5, 10, 15, 20)
4. Add the neighbor condition to explore state too
5. Try C=4 (below threshold) in stop to return to explore when few neighbors (isolated = maybe pre-switch)
6. Different combination of transitions in stop state

Note: cle values must be 0, 4, 5, 6 (not 2!). Correcting from previous error.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of round 6:**

The pattern is very clear now. The best machines all share:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1, cle=4), 2 transitions: green detection (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) + random stop (C=5, p=0.001)
- State 1: Stop (cle=6), 2 transitions: random return (C=5, p=0.001) + neighbor-above-threshold (C=3, p=threshold, w=15)

**Key findings on the neighbor threshold in stop state:**
- p=3 → 7595 (bad - too low, fires too often)
- p=4 → 6736 (worse than champion)
- p=5 → 6351 (champion-level but slightly worse in this round)
- p=6 → 6245 (best this round!)
- p=7 → 6249 (second best this round!)
- The champion (p=5, w=15) scored 6237 in round 5, but 6351 in round 6 (stochastic variation)

So the optimal threshold appears to be around 6-7 neighbors. Higher thresholds (6, 7) are better than lower ones (3, 4, 5). Let me explore p=8, 9, 10 as well, and vary steepness w.

**What does this mechanism do?** When a robot accidentally stops pre-switch (via p=0.001 random), if ≥6 of its neighbors are moving (everyone else is still exploring), it returns to exploring. This corrects pre-switch mistakes. A threshold of 6 means: "if most neighbors are moving (pre-switch state), resume moving." Post-switch, once many robots have stopped, fewer neighbors are moving, so the condition rarely fires → robot stays stopped.

**Explorations for round 7:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. p=6, w=15 (best this round) - slight variations
3. p=7, w=15 - slight variations  
4. p=8, w=15
5. p=9, w=15
6. p=10, w=15
7. p=6, w=10 and p=6, w=20 (vary steepness)
8. p=7, w=10 and p=7, w=20
9. Adding 3rd transition to stop state
10. Varying the random return prob from stop (p=0.001)

The threshold w (steepness): higher w = sharper sigmoid = more binary threshold behavior. Since we want a clean threshold at the neighbor count, higher w should be better.

Let me also try: what if we use C=4 (below threshold) instead of C=3 (above threshold) in stop? C=4 fires when neighbors BELOW threshold - this would keep robot in stop when few neighbors are around (post-switch, isolated), and return to explore when many neighbors. Actually that's the same logic as C=3 above threshold...

Wait, C=3 above threshold p=6 w=15: fires when >6 neighbors → return to explore. This fires pre-switch (many moving neighbors) = good correction. Post-switch, robots spread out, few neighbors → doesn't fire → stays stopped. ✓

C=4 below threshold p=6 w=15: fires when <6 neighbors → return to explore. This fires post-switch (few neighbors = spread out) = bad. ✗

So C=3 is the right choice.

**Machines:**

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of round 7:**

The champion is: `rwm=1, cle=4, random p=0.002 (explore→stop), green detection p=1.0` → Stop with `random p=0.001 + neighbor-above-6, w=15`. Score: 6094.12.

**Key finding**: Increasing the random transition probability from explore to stop from p=0.001 to p=0.002 improved the score! This seems counterintuitive (more random stopping pre-switch = more penalty), but perhaps it creates more "scouts" in stop state that can re-enter explore via the neighbor condition (if many neighbors still moving), effectively creating a distributed voting mechanism.

**Parameter landscape:**
- Random prob from explore (p0x1): 0.001 → worse, 0.002 → champion. Try 0.003, 0.004, 0.005
- Neighbor threshold in stop (p1x1): 5→worst, 6→medium, 7→medium, 8-10→good. But champion uses 6 with p0x1=0.002. Maybe with higher random prob, lower threshold is better?
- Steepness w: 10, 15, 20 all similar. Try higher values.
- The 3-transition stop state was worst (rank 13) - avoid

**What to explore:**
1. Champion (exact copy) - p0x1=0.002, threshold=6, w=15
2. p0x1=0.003, threshold=6, w=15
3. p0x1=0.004, threshold=6, w=15
4. p0x1=0.005, threshold=6, w=15
5. p0x1=0.002, threshold=7, w=15
6. p0x1=0.002, threshold=8, w=15
7. p0x1=0.002, threshold=6, w=20
8. p0x1=0.003, threshold=7, w=15
9. p0x1=0.003, threshold=8, w=15
10. p0x1=0.002, threshold=6, w=15, but vary stop return prob (p1x0=0.0005 or 0.002)
11. p0x1=0.002, threshold=5, w=15 (re-test with new p0x1)
12. p0x1=0.002, threshold=6, w=15, different cle0/cle1
13. Exploration: try 3-state with two explore speeds

The key pattern is the same 2-state structure. The main parameters to tune are:
- Random transition prob from explore state (currently optimal ~0.002)
- Neighbor threshold in stop state (currently optimal ~6-8)
- Steepness w (15-20 seem good)

Let me also think about whether the stop state's return-to-explore probability (p1x0=0.001) could be optimized. Lower might be better (robots that stopped post-switch should stay stopped). But the neighbor condition already handles the pre-switch case.

Also: the random prob p0x1 in explore state: 0.002 per step over 500 steps → P(at least one trigger) per robot ≈ 63%. With 20 robots, expect ~12-13 to randomly enter stop state before switch. With threshold=6 in stop, they re-enter explore if ≥6 neighbors visible (range 0.5m). Since robots start clustered in center, many will have ≥6 neighbors initially, so they bounce back quickly. As time passes and robots spread, fewer neighbors → more stay stopped. This might actually help coverage!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of round 8:**

The champion is: `p0x1=0.002, p1x0=0.0005, threshold=6, w=15`. Score: 5766.76.

**Key finding**: Reducing the stop→explore random return probability from 0.001 to 0.0005 (5e-04) gave a significant improvement! Lower return probability = robots that stopped post-switch stay stopped longer. Let me explore even lower values.

**Parameter landscape:**
- p0x1 (explore→stop random): 0.002 is optimal (0.001 worse in prev rounds, 0.003+ worse)
- p1x0 (stop→explore random): 0.0005 is new best (0.001 was previous). Try 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003
- Neighbor threshold p1x1: 6-8 seem good with p0x1=0.002
- w: 15-20 seem good

**What drives the improvement?**
- Lower p1x0 = robots that stop (pre-switch via random or post-switch via green detection) are less likely to return to exploring
- The neighbor condition (C=3, threshold=6) already handles pre-switch correction (many moving neighbors → return to explore)
- So p1x0 (random return) is redundant for pre-switch correction and only hurts post-switch
- Therefore, minimize p1x0 as much as possible

**Try:** p1x0 = 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003, 0.0005

Also explore:
- Combining lower p1x0 with different thresholds
- Whether we can drop p1x0 further or eliminate it somehow (but must have at least 1 transition with p>0)
- The neighbor threshold: with p1x0=0.0005, what's optimal threshold?

Note: p parameter for C=5 must be in [0,1] and is a probability. The minimum meaningful value is essentially 0 (but > 0). Let me try 0.0001.

Also: could we make the stop state have ONLY the neighbor condition (no random return)? We need at least 1 transition but C=3 alone would suffice. If the sigmoid is very steep and threshold very high (like 10), the neighbor condition almost never fires, effectively eliminating return from stop. But when pre-switch random stop happens with many neighbors, it DOES fire and sends robot back to explore. This could be better than having p1x0=0.0001.

Let me try: stop state with only C=3 (neighbor above threshold) transition, no random.

Actually wait - we need `--n1 T` with T>=1. We can have just 1 transition being C=3. Let me try that.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of round 9:**

The champion is: `p0x1=0.002, stop state with ONLY C=3 neighbor condition (threshold=7, w=20)`. Score: 5383.12.

**Critical finding**: Removing the random return transition (p1x0) from the stop state entirely (using only C=3 neighbor condition) gave the best result! The stop state with only 1 transition (C=3, threshold=7, w=20) scores 5383 vs 5399 (threshold=6, w=15). 

**Why this works so well**: 
- The only way to leave stop state is if ≥7 neighbors are visible (within 0.5m range)
- Pre-switch: a robot that randomly stopped (p=0.002) will see many moving neighbors clustered nearby → C=3 fires → returns to explore ✓
- Post-switch: robots spread out (rwm=1 = straight lines), few within 0.5m → C=3 doesn't fire → stays stopped ✓
- No random return (no spurious exits post-switch) ✓

**Parameters to optimize for the final round:**
1. Neighbor threshold (p1x0 in C=3): 6 scored 5399, 7 scored 5383. Try 8, 9, 10
2. Steepness w: 15 scored 5399 (threshold=6), 20 scored 5383 (threshold=7). Try higher w values
3. p0x1 (explore→stop random): 0.002 is the sweet spot. Keep it.
4. Combinations: threshold=8, w=20; threshold=7, w=15; threshold=8, w=15; threshold=9, w=20; threshold=10, w=20; threshold=7, w=20 (champion)

Also consider:
- What if we add a second transition to stop (C=3 with different params) to make it more robust?
- What about varying the green detection probability in explore (currently p=1.0)?
- What about p0x1 variations (0.0015, 0.0025)?

The scores are converging. The main axis of improvement is:
- Threshold: higher = harder to leave stop post-switch (good) but might fail to release pre-switch accidentally stopped robots (bad if they have too few neighbors). With 20 robots and 0.5m range in center, initially many have ≥7 neighbors.
- w (steepness): higher = sharper threshold = cleaner behavior

Let me focus on the 1-transition stop state with C=3, varying threshold and w:

**Machines:**
1. Champion (exact copy) - threshold=7, w=20
2. threshold=8, w=20
3. threshold=9, w=20
4. threshold=10, w=20
5. threshold=6, w=20
6. threshold=7, w=15 (was rank 2 last round but needs re-test with this exact topology... actually rank 2 was threshold=6, w=15)
7. threshold=8, w=15
8. threshold=7, w=20 with p0x1=0.0015
9. threshold=7, w=20 with p0x1=0.0025
10. threshold=6, w=20 (same as #6 above... let me adjust)
11. threshold=7, w=20 - duplicate champion for robustness (extra evaluation)
12. threshold=5, w=20 (explore lower end)

Actually, let me be more systematic. The champion has threshold=7, w=20. Second best had threshold=6, w=15. The gap between 1st and 2nd is small (5383 vs 5399 = ~16 pts). Let me explore more carefully:

- threshold: 5,6,7(champ),8,9,10
- w: 15, 20
- p0x1: 0.0015, 0.002(champ), 0.0025

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
